 L_y\le2\left(\frac{W}{s_n}\right)^{2/n}<2
                    \qquad\text{for $\nu$-almost every }y.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-chord-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-chord-density</summary>

*Proof.* Use the disjoint chart representation of Proposition <a href="#prop:chart-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:chart-mass">8</a>. For one chart $\phi_i:E_i\to Z_i\subset Y$, write $$u_i=v\circ\phi_i,
 \qquad \rho_i=\lvert\theta_i\rvert J_i,
 \qquad J_i=\sqrt{\det G_i},$$ and extend $u_i$ and $\rho_i$ by zero to $\mathbb R^n$. The bi-Lipschitz bounds give a positive lower bound for $J_i$ on this chart almost everywhere. Since $\lvert\theta_i\rvert\ge1$, $v\in L^p(\mu)$ and $\mu(Y)<\infty$, $u_i$ is Lebesgue integrable. The function $\rho_i$ is integrable as well, by the mass representation. Thus ordinary Lebesgue differentiation applies to both functions and to $1_{E_i}$.

<a id="chord:lebesgue-values"></a>
For $\nu$-almost every $y=\phi_i(z)$, we may therefore require all of the following: $z$ is a density point of $E_i$; the centered metric differential is the Euclidean norm $\lvert\cdot\rvert_{G_0}$, with $G_0=G_i(z)>0$; and $u_i,\rho_i$ have Lebesgue values $$\label{chord:lebesgue-values}
 v_0=v(y)>0,
 \qquad \rho_0=\lvert\theta_i(z)\rvert\sqrt{\det G_0}>0.$$ Indeed the exceptional coordinate sets are Lebesgue-null and therefore have zero chart mass, while $\{v=0\}$ has zero $\nu$-mass. There are only countably many charts. The representative of $v$ agrees with its Lebesgue value at almost every such point. Fix a center with these properties.

Let $\ell=\liminf_{a\to\infty}a^{n/2}J_y(a)$. If $\ell=\infty$, the desired lower bound is automatic. Otherwise, choose $a_j\to\infty$ realizing this lower limit and put $\varepsilon_j=a_j^{-1/2}$. On every bounded set of $h\in\mathbb R^n$, the functions $$u_i(z+\varepsilon_jh),\qquad
 \rho_i(z+\varepsilon_jh),\qquad
 1_{E_i}(z+\varepsilon_jh)$$ converge in measure to $v_0,\rho_0,1$, respectively. For example, this follows by changing variables in the defining mean convergence at the Lebesgue point $z$. A diagonal subsequence over bounded balls makes all three convergences hold almost everywhere in $\mathbb R^n$. The subsequence still realizes $\ell$.

<a id="chord:rescaled-distance"></a>
At almost every $h$, the points $z+\varepsilon_jh$ consequently belong to $E_i$ for all sufficiently large $j$. The centered metric differential then gives $$\label{chord:rescaled-distance}
 R_j(h):=\varepsilon_j^{-1}
          d\bigl(y,\phi_i(z+\varepsilon_jh)\bigr)
                    \mathrel{\begingroup
  \let\hookrightarrowP PaperOriginalhookrightarrow\endcsname
  \let\mapstoP PaperOriginalmapsto\endcsname
  \let\longmapstoP PaperOriginallongmapsto\endcsname
  \let\longrightarrowP PaperOriginallongrightarrow\endcsname
  \let\LongrightarrowP PaperOriginalLongrightarrow\endcsname
  \let\LongleftrightarrowP PaperOriginalLongleftrightarrow\endcsname
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27F6> >> BDC}%
  PPaperOriginallongrightarrow\endcsname\pdfliteral direct{EMC}\endgroup}\lvert h\rvert_{G_0}.$$ When the chart point is absent, define the integrand below to be zero. Restricting the nonnegative integral defining $J_y$ to this chart and changing variables gives, for every fixed bounded ball $D_R\subset\mathbb R^n$ centered at zero, $$W a_j^{n/2}J_y(a_j)
 \ge\int_{D_R}
 \frac{1_{E_i}(z+\varepsilon_jh)
        u_i(z+\varepsilon_jh)^p\rho_i(z+\varepsilon_jh)}
      {\bigl(1+\kappa^2R_j(h)^2
                   u_i(z+\varepsilon_jh)^{2\beta}\bigr)^n}\,dh.$$ The factor $a_j^{n/2}$ has canceled the Jacobian $\varepsilon_j^n$. Fatou’s lemma, <a href="#chord:lebesgue-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="chord:lebesgue-values">[chord:lebesgue-values]</a>, and <a href="#chord:rescaled-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="chord:rescaled-distance">[chord:rescaled-distance]</a> now imply $$W\ell\ge v_0^p\rho_0
        \int_{D_R}\bigl(1+v_0^{4\beta}\lvert h\rvert_{G_0}^2\bigr)^{-n}
                 \,dh.$$ This step uses no uniform integrability of the rescaled densities. Letting $R\to\infty$ and applying monotone convergence gives the same bound with $D_R$ replaced by $\mathbb R^n$.

The linear substitution $H=v_0^{2\beta}G_0^{1/2}h$ has Jacobian $v_0^{2n\beta}\sqrt{\det G_0}$. Since $2n\beta=p$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 W\ell
 &\ge \lvert\theta_i(z)\rvert
                \int_{\mathbb R^n}(1+\lvert H\rvert^2)^{-n}\,dH\\
 &=\lvert\theta_i(z)\rvert\,2^{-n}s_n
 \ge2^{-n}s_n.
\end{aligned}$$ The middle equality is the stereographic integral <a href="#eq:stereographic-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stereographic-integral">[eq:stereographic-integral]</a>. The final inequality uses the nonzero integer multiplicity $\lvert\theta_i(z)\rvert\ge1$. This proves <a href="#chord:transform-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="chord:transform-density">[chord:transform-density]</a> without a bound on the multiplicity.

If $L_y>0$, Proposition <a href="#prop:chord-transform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:chord-transform">25</a> and <a href="#chord:transform-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="chord:transform-density">[chord:transform-density]</a> give $$\frac{s_n}{2^nW}\le(2L_y)^{-n/2}.$$ Rearranging yields <a href="#eq:chord-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chord-upper">[eq:chord-upper]</a>. If $L_y=0$, that upper bound is immediate. Its strict inequality follows from $W<s_n$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-chord-sphere"></a>
<div id="rem:chord-sphere" class="remark">

*Remark 27* (The round sphere). The constants can be checked simultaneously in every dimension on the unit round $S^n\subset\mathbb R^{n+1}$ with $v=1$. Here $W=s_n$, $\nu$ is normalized area, and $L_y=2$ by rotational symmetry. In stereographic coordinates chosen so that $\lvert x-y\rvert^2=4\lvert z\rvert^2/(1+\lvert z\rvert^2)$, $$J_y(a)=\frac{2^n}{s_n}
     \int_{\mathbb R^n}\bigl(1+(1+4a)\lvert z\rvert^2\bigr)^{-n}\,dz
          =(1+4a)^{-n/2}.$$ Thus the transform estimate is an equality, and its limiting coefficient is $2^{-n}$, exactly the value in <a href="#chord:transform-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="chord:transform-density">[chord:transform-density]</a> when $W=s_n$.

</div>

Only the scalar values $v(y),v(x)$ and the distance $d(y,x)$ occur in <a href="#eq:chord-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chord-upper">[eq:chord-upper]</a>. The kernel $$(y,x)\mathrel{\begingroup
  \let\hookrightarrowP PaperOriginalhookrightarrow\endcsname
  \let\mapstoP PaperOriginalmapsto\endcsname
  \let\longmapstoP PaperOriginallongmapsto\endcsname
  \let\longrightarrowP PaperOriginallongrightarrow\endcsname
  \let\LongrightarrowP PaperOriginalLongrightarrow\endcsname
  \let\LongleftrightarrowP PaperOriginalLongleftrightarrow\endcsname
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27FC> >> BDC}%
  PPaperOriginallongmapsto\endcsname\pdfliteral direct{EMC}\endgroup}
          v(y)^{2\beta}d(y,x)^2v(x)^{2\beta}$$ is Borel and is integrable against $\nu\otimes\nu$, since $Y$ has finite diameter and $v^{p+2\beta}\in L^1(\mu)$. Accordingly, the almost-everywhere upper bound can be averaged without any jointly measurable choice of the covectors $D_xd(y,x)$.

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Averaging and completion of the proof

We first derive the lower bound that contradicts the chord estimate of Section <a href="#sec:chords" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:chords">6</a>. Throughout this argument, $n>2$, and we retain the normalized extremizing cycle $A$, its mass measure $\mu$, and the nonnegative minimizer $v$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:minimizer">20</a>. Thus $$\beta=\frac1{n-2},\qquad p=\frac{2n}{n-2}=2+4\beta,
 \qquad W=\int_Y v^p\,d\mu>0,
 \qquad \,d\nu=\frac{v^p}{W}\,d\mu.$$ We use the same finite, nonnegative Borel representative of $v$ as in Section <a href="#sec:chords" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:chords">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-opposite-average"></a>
<div id="lem:opposite-average" class="lemma">

<a id="finish:weighted-moment"></a>
<a id="finish:all-centers"></a>
<a id="finish:opposite-average"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Opposite averaged chord bound). *Define $$\label{finish:weighted-moment}
 B_*:=\int_Y v^{p+2\beta}\,d\mu,
 \qquad
 I(y):=\int_Y d(y,x)^2v(x)^{p+2\beta}\,d\mu(x).$$ Then $0<B_*<\infty$, the function $I$ is finite and continuous on $Y$, and $$\label{finish:all-centers}
 W^2\le B_*I(y)\qquad\text{for every }y\in Y.$$ In particular, for the chord moments $$L_y=\int_Y
       \bigl[v(y)^\beta d(y,x)v(x)^\beta\bigr]^2\,d\nu(x),$$ one has $$\label{finish:opposite-average}
 \int_Y L_y\,d\nu(y)\ge2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-opposite-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-opposite-average</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:powers">21</a> gives all finite moments of $v$, so $B_*$ is finite. It is positive because $W>0$. Write $D_Y=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}Y$. Then $0\le I(y)\le D_Y^2B_*$, and the triangle inequality gives $$|I(y)-I(z)|\le2D_YB_*d(y,z)\qquad(y,z\in Y).$$ This proves the stated continuity.

<a id="finish:radial-test"></a>
<a id="finish:cauchy"></a>
Fix any $y\in Y$ and put $r=d(y,\cdot)$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:powers">21</a>, the nonnegative function $g=v^p$ belongs to $\mathcal H$, and $Dg=pv^{p-1}Dv$. The extension of the radial inequality <a href="#eq:radial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:radial">[eq:radial]</a> furnished by Proposition <a href="#prop:sobolev-space" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sobolev-space">18</a> therefore applies to this $g$. Dividing by $n$ and using $p/n=2\beta$ gives $$\label{finish:radial-test}
 W\le\int_Y r\left(v^p\sqrt{1-|Dr|^2}
             -2\beta v^{p-1}\langle Dr,Dv\rangle\right)\,d\mu.$$ At almost every point of each current chart, take the direct sum of its cotangent inner-product space with $\mathbb R$. The vector $$\left(\sqrt{1-|Dr|^2},Dr\right)$$ has norm one. Moreover, the identities $$\frac p2+\beta+1+\beta=p,
 \qquad
 \frac p2+\beta+\beta=p-1$$ give the factorization $$\begin{aligned}
 &v^p\sqrt{1-|Dr|^2}-2\beta v^{p-1}\langle Dr,Dv\rangle\\
 &\quad=
 v^{p/2+\beta}
 \left\langle
   \left(\sqrt{1-|Dr|^2},Dr\right),
   \left(v^{1+\beta},-2\beta v^\beta Dv\right)
 \right\rangle\\
 &\quad\le
 v^{p/2+\beta}
 \left(v^{2+2\beta}+4\beta^2v^{2\beta}|Dv|^2\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ All powers in this formula are nonnegative powers of $v$, and the identity also holds on $\{v=0\}$. Applying the integral Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to <a href="#finish:radial-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:radial-test">[finish:radial-test]</a> now yields $$\label{finish:cauchy}
 W^2\le I(y)
 \int_Y\left(v^{2+2\beta}
             +4\beta^2v^{2\beta}|Dv|^2\right)\,d\mu.$$ Both factors are finite: the first was bounded above, and the second is finite by the moment estimates and the Sobolev power $v^{1+\beta}$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:powers">21</a>.

<a id="finish:euler-moment"></a>
To identify the last integral, use $\psi=v^{1+2\beta}\in\mathcal H$ in the Euler equation <a href="#eq:euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler">[eq:euler]</a>. Its gradient is $D\psi=(1+2\beta)v^{2\beta}Dv$. Thus $$4\beta(1+2\beta)\int_Yv^{2\beta}|Dv|^2\,d\mu
 +n\int_Yv^{2+2\beta}\,d\mu
 =n\int_Yv^{p+2\beta}\,d\mu.$$ Since $1+2\beta=n\beta$, division by $n$ proves $$\label{finish:euler-moment}
 \int_Y\left(v^{2+2\beta}
             +4\beta^2v^{2\beta}|Dv|^2\right)\,d\mu=B_*.$$ Equations <a href="#finish:cauchy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:cauchy">[finish:cauchy]</a> and <a href="#finish:euler-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:euler-moment">[finish:euler-moment]</a> prove <a href="#finish:all-centers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:all-centers">[finish:all-centers]</a>. The argument fixed an arbitrary center before taking chart derivatives. Hence its conclusion holds for every center, although the null set on which $Dr$ is undefined may depend on that center.

<a id="finish:variance"></a>
We have proved the all-center estimate $W^2\le B_*I(y)$. It remains to combine it with the CAT(0) variance inequality (Sturm 2003, Proposition 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Sturm2003](#ref-Sturm2003)], which doubles this lower contribution when we average over centers. We recall its proof for the finite measure $v^{p+2\beta}\mu$. Compactness of $Y$ and continuity of $I$ provide a minimizer $o\in Y$. This is a barycenter of the finite measure $v^{p+2\beta}\mu$. For $y\in Y$, let $o_t$ be the point at fraction $t\in(0,1)$ on the segment from $o$ to $y$. The CAT(0) squared-distance inequality, integrated against $v(x)^{p+2\beta}\,d\mu(x)$, gives $$I(o)\le I(o_t)
 \le(1-t)I(o)+tI(y)-t(1-t)B_*d(o,y)^2.$$ Subtracting $(1-t)I(o)$, dividing by $t$, and letting $t\downarrow0$ proves the variance inequality $$\label{finish:variance}
 I(y)\ge I(o)+B_*d(o,y)^2\qquad(y\in Y).$$ This uses only the segment from $o$ to $y$.

The chord kernel is a nonnegative Borel function on $Y\times Y$. Its integral is finite, since its expression below is bounded above by $D_Y^2B_*^2/W^2$. Tonelli’s theorem and <a href="#finish:weighted-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:weighted-moment">[finish:weighted-moment]</a> give <a id="finish:double-integral"></a>$$\label{finish:double-integral}
 \begin{aligned}
 \int_YL_y\,d\nu(y)
 &=\iint_{Y\times Y}
       \bigl[v(y)^\beta d(y,x)v(x)^\beta\bigr]^2
             \,d\nu(x)\,d\nu(y)\\
 &=\frac1{W^2}\int_Y I(y)v(y)^{p+2\beta}\,d\mu(y)\\
 &\ge\frac1{W^2}\left(
       B_*I(o)+B_*\int_Y d(o,y)^2v(y)^{p+2\beta}\,d\mu(y)
                  \right)\\
 &=\frac{2B_*I(o)}{W^2}\ge2.
 \end{aligned}$$ Here the first inequality is <a href="#finish:variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:variance">[finish:variance]</a>, and the last is <a href="#finish:all-centers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:all-centers">[finish:all-centers]</a> at $y=o$. The averaged expressions contain only distances and values of the Borel representative of $v$; the proof requires no joint choice of the derivatives of distance functions. This proves <a href="#finish:opposite-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:opposite-average">[finish:opposite-average]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-opposite-average-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-opposite-average</summary>

<a id="finish:compact-bound"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* We first prove the statement in every compact CAT(0) space by induction on the cycle dimension $n\ge2$. For such a space $Y$, let $V(T)$ denote the infimum of the masses of integral fillings of an integral $n$-cycle $T$ in $Y$. Theorem <a href="#thm:current-background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:current-background">4</a> ensures that this infimum is finite and attained. It therefore suffices in each dimension to prove $$\label{finish:compact-bound}
 V(T)\le c_n\mathbf M(T)^{(n+1)/n}
 \qquad\text{for every integral }n\text{-cycle }T\text{ in }Y.$$ The zero cycle has the zero current as a filling.

In dimension $n=2$, a violation of <a href="#finish:compact-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:compact-bound">[finish:compact-bound]</a> would, by Lemma <a href="#lem:extremizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:extremizer">5</a>, produce a nonzero extremizing cycle that can be rescaled to satisfy <a href="#eq:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalization">[eq:normalization]</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:base" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:base">11</a> excludes this cycle: its radial inequality and Euclidean lower density force $\mathbf M(A)\ge s_2$, whereas the normalization requires $\mathbf M(A)<s_2$. Thus <a href="#finish:compact-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:compact-bound">[finish:compact-bound]</a> holds in dimension two, and attainment gives an integral filling with that mass bound.

Now let $n>2$, and assume the asserted existence of sharp integral fillings in dimension $n-1$ in every compact CAT(0) space. Suppose that <a href="#finish:compact-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:compact-bound">[finish:compact-bound]</a> fails for a cycle $T$ in a compact CAT(0) space $Y$. Since $T\ne0$, we may choose $$c_n<c<\frac{V(T)}{\mathbf M(T)^{(n+1)/n}}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:extremizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:extremizer">5</a> produces a nonzero cycle $A$ maximizing $V(B)-c\mathbf M(B)^{(n+1)/n}$ over integral $n$-cycles. After a constant rescaling of the metric it satisfies <a href="#eq:extremizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extremizer">[eq:extremizer]</a> and <a href="#eq:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalization">[eq:normalization]</a>, in particular $$m:=\mathbf M(A)=((n+1)c)^{-n}<s_n.$$ The rescaled space remains compact and CAT(0), so the induction hypothesis continues to apply there.

Proposition <a href="#prop:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:radial">10</a> gives <a href="#eq:radial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:radial">[eq:radial]</a> for this cycle. The sharp filling statement in dimension $n-1$ is exactly the induction input to Proposition <a href="#prop:small-set" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:small-set">12</a>. Consequently Section <a href="#sec:sobolev" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sobolev">4</a>, and in particular the almost sharp Sobolev inequality of Proposition <a href="#prop:almost-sobolev" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:almost-sobolev">17</a>, applies. Propositions <a href="#prop:sobolev-space" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sobolev-space">18</a> and <a href="#prop:compact-embedding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:compact-embedding">19</a> supply the completed calculus and compactness used in Proposition <a href="#prop:minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:minimizer">20</a>. We therefore obtain a nonnegative minimizer $v$ satisfying <a href="#eq:euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:euler">[eq:euler]</a> and <a href="#eq:minimizer-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:minimizer-normalization">[eq:minimizer-normalization]</a>, with $$0<W=\int_Yv^p\,d\mu<s_n.$$ Lemmas <a href="#lem:powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:powers">21</a> and <a href="#lem:composites" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:composites">22</a> justify the powers and nonlinear tests used in Section <a href="#sec:chords" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:chords">6</a> and in Lemma <a href="#lem:opposite-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:opposite-average">28</a>.

For the probability measure $\nu=v^p\mu/W$, the chord upper bound <a href="#eq:chord-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chord-upper">[eq:chord-upper]</a> holds for $\nu$-almost every center. Integrating that bound and using Lemma <a href="#lem:opposite-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:opposite-average">28</a> gives $$2\le\int_YL_y\,d\nu(y)
   \le2\left(\frac W{s_n}\right)^{2/n}<2,$$ a contradiction. Hence no violating cycle $T$ exists, and <a href="#finish:compact-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="finish:compact-bound">[finish:compact-bound]</a> holds in dimension $n$. By attainment in Theorem <a href="#thm:current-background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:current-background">4</a>, every integral $n$-cycle has an integral filling realizing $V(T)$, with the required mass bound. Its support is compact because it is a closed subset of $Y$. This completes the induction for every $n\ge2$.

Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-reduction">3</a> now transfers this bound to every compactly supported integral cycle $T\in\mathbf I_n(X)$ in a proper CAT(0) space. It supplies a compactly supported integral filling $S$ with $$\partial S=T,\qquad
 \mathbf M(S)\le c_n\mathbf M(T)^{(n+1)/n}
 =\frac{\mathbf M(T)^{(n+1)/n}}
 {(n+1)^{(n+1)/n}\omega_{n+1}^{1/n}}.$$ The zero cycle has the zero filling. This proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:classical"></a>
### The classical domain inequality and sharpness

We prove Corollary <a href="#cor:cartan-hadamard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:cartan-hadamard">2</a> and verify the optimality of the filling coefficient. Both conclusions use the absence of compactly supported top-dimensional cycles in a connected noncompact oriented manifold.

<a id="stmt-lem-top-dimensional-constancy"></a>
<div id="lem:top-dimensional-constancy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29**. *Let $M$ be a connected, noncompact, oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension $d$. If $C\in\mathbf I_d(M)$ has compact support and $\partial C=0$, then $C=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-top-dimensional-constancy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-top-dimensional-constancy</summary>

*Proof.* In a relatively compact oriented coordinate ball, the top-dimensional representation of an integral current is integration against an integer-valued locally integrable coefficient $\theta$. Testing $\partial C=0$ with compactly supported smooth $(d-1)$-forms shows that every distributional partial derivative of $\theta$ is zero. Thus $\theta$ is constant almost everywhere on that ball. For completeness, convolution with a smooth kernel gives functions with zero ordinary gradient on each smaller ball; passing to the local $L^1$ limit proves the assertion. Compatibility on overlaps and connectedness give one constant on $M$. Some nonempty open set lies outside the compact support, so this constant is zero. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-top-dimensional-constancy-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-top-dimensional-constancy</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:cartan-hadamard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:cartan-hadamard">2</a>.* By the Cartan–Hadamard theorem and Hopf–Rinow, $M$ is a proper CAT(0) space and is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb R^d$; in particular it is oriented and noncompact. See (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, vol. 319, secs. I.3, II.1A and II.4) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999)] for the metric comparison and completeness statements. Let $[[\Omega]]$ be its integration current. Its mass is $\operatorname{vol}(\Omega)$, and the mass of $T=\partial[[\Omega]]$ is $\operatorname{area}(\partial\Omega)$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, with $n=d-1$, gives a compactly supported $S\in\mathbf I_d(M)$ with $\partial S=T$ and $$\mathbf M(S)\le
 \frac{\operatorname{area}(\partial\Omega)^{d/(d-1)}}
 {d^{d/(d-1)}\omega_d^{1/(d-1)}}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:top-dimensional-constancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:top-dimensional-constancy">29</a> applies to $S-[[\Omega]]$, so $S=[[\Omega]]$. Rearranging its mass bound proves <a href="#eq:classical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:classical">[eq:classical]</a>.

For a relatively compact set $\Omega$ of finite perimeter, the standard representation of its integration current gives $[[\Omega]]\in\mathbf I_d(M)$, with mass $\operatorname{vol}(\Omega)$ and boundary mass equal to its perimeter; see (Simon 2014, chap. 3, Section 4, Theorem 4.3; Chapter 6, Section 3, Remark 3.15) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Simon2014](#ref-Simon2014)]. The same argument applies verbatim. ◻

</details>

For $d=2$, the classical disk inequality gives $L(\partial D)^2\ge4\pi\operatorname{area}(D)$ for a smooth Riemannian disk of Gaussian curvature at most zero; see (Izmestiev 2014, sec. 1.2, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Izmestiev2014](#ref-Izmestiev2014)] for this formulation. Filling the holes in a bounded smooth domain on a Cartan–Hadamard surface increases area and decreases boundary length. Applying the disk inequality to each resulting component, and then summing, proves the same bound for the domain because $(\sum_j L_j)^2\ge\sum_jL_j^2$. Together with Corollary <a href="#cor:cartan-hadamard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:cartan-hadamard">2</a>, this establishes the Euclidean Cartan–Hadamard domain inequality in every ambient dimension.

To see that the coefficient in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is optimal, take $X=\mathbb R^{n+1}$ and let $T$ be the oriented boundary of the Euclidean ball $B_R$. Its mass is $s_nR^n$, while $$\mathbf M([[B_R]])=\omega_{n+1}R^{n+1}
 =c_n(s_nR^n)^{(n+1)/n}.$$ Any compactly supported integral filling of $T$ equals $[[B_R]]$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:top-dimensional-constancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:top-dimensional-constancy">29</a>. Hence equality occurs and no smaller universal coefficient is possible.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Radial variation in the whole-current product and its Euclidean comparison triangle. Panel (a) distinguishes a fixed-time slice from the swept current; the stack is symbolic and makes no regularity or injectivity assertion about its image in $Y$. In panel (b), the barred vertices correspond to $y, x, x′$; $p$ and $p′$ lie at fractions $λ$ and $λ′$ on their radial sides. The fractions may differ even at the same time because $g$ varies with the point. The comparison distance gives the upper bound [eq:radial-two-point]. The exact determinant in [eq:radial-total-jacobian] belongs to the quadratic upper form, rather than an asserted exact differential of the deformed map.",
      "figure_id": "fig:radial-variation",
      "pdf_page": 14,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:radial-variation",
          "exact_quote": "Radial variation in the whole-current product and its Euclidean\ncomparison triangle. Panel (a) distinguishes a fixed-time slice from\nthe swept current; the stack is symbolic and makes no regularity or\ninjectivity assertion about its image i",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Latex,font=\\small]\n  \\begin{scope}[xshift=0cm]\n    \\node[anchor=west,font=\\bfseries\\small] at (-.3,3.4) {(a) Slice and sweep};\n    \\draw[fill=blue!5,draw=blue!45!black] (0,.2) rectangle (2.6,2.5);\n    \\foreach \\yy in {.2,.66,1.12,1.58,2.04,2.5}\n      \\draw[blue!30] (0,\\yy)--(2.6,\\yy);\n    \\draw[very thick,blue!65!black] (0,1.58)--(2.6,1.58);\n    \\draw[->] (-.35,.1)--(-.35,2.7) node[above] {$t$};\n    \\node[left] at (-.35,.2) {$0$};\n    \\node[left] at (-.35,2.5) {$h$};\n    \\node at (1.3,2.2) {product};\n    \\node at (1.3,.9) {$[0,h]\\times A$};\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (2.75,1.58) {$\\{t\\}\\times A$};\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (0,-1.7) {$\\{t\\}\\times A\\mapsto(F_t)_\\#A$};\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (0,-.2) {$B_h=(F_h)_\\#A$};\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (0,-.7) {$D_h=F_\\#([0,h]\\times A)$};\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (0,-1.2) {$\\partial D_h=B_h-A$};\n  \\end{scope}\n  \\begin{scope}[xshift=6.0cm]\n    \\node[anchor=west,font=\\bfseries\\small] at (-.1,3.4) {(b) Unequal fractions};\n    \\coordinate (y) at (0,.15);\n    \\coordinate (x) at (3.7,.15);\n    \\coordinate (xp) at (2.4,2.55);\n    \\coordinate (p) at ($(y)!.73!(x)$);\n    \\coordinate (pp) at ($(y)!.53!(xp)$);\n    \\draw[black!60] (y)--(x)--(xp)--cycle;\n    \\draw[very thick,blue!65!black] (p)--(pp);\n    \\foreach \\pt in {y,x,xp,p,pp} \\fill (\\pt) circle (1.4pt);\n    \\node[below left] at (y) {$\\bar y$};\n    \\node[below right] at (x) {$\\bar x$};\n    \\node[above] at (xp) {$\\bar x'$};\n    \\node[below] at (p) {$p$};\n    \\node[left] at (pp) {$p'$};\n    \\node[below] at ($(y)!.36!(x)$) {$\\lambda r$};\n    \\node[above left] at ($(y)!.27!(xp)$) {$\\lambda'r'$};\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (0,-.65) {$\\lambda=1-tg(x),\\quad\\lambda'=1-tg(x')$};\n    \\node[anchor=west] at (0,-1.2) {$d(F_t(x),F_t(x'))\\le |p-p'|$};\n  \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two panels side by side:\nPanel (a) titled \"(a) Slice and sweep\" depicts a vertical axis labeled $t$ with marked values $0$ and $h$ and an upward arrow. A shaded rectangular prism representing the cylinder $[0, h] \\times A$ is labeled \"product\" and \"$[0, h] \\times A$\". A purple horizontal cross-section at height $t$ is labeled \"$\\{t\\} \\times A$\". Below the diagram are listed:\n$B_h = (F_h)_\\# A$\n$D_h = F_\\#([0, h] \\times A)$\n$\\partial D_h = B_h - A$\n$\\{t\\} \\times A \\mapsto (F_t)_\\# A$\nPanel (b) titled \"(b) Unequal fractions\" shows a Euclidean comparison triangle with vertices $\\bar{y}$ (bottom left), $\\bar{x}$ (bottom right), and $\\bar{x}'$ (top right). A point $p$ lies on the bottom edge $\\bar{y}\\bar{x}$ labeled $\\lambda r$, and a point $p'$ lies on the edge $\\bar{y}\\bar{x}'$ labeled $\\lambda' r'$. A blue segment connects $p$ to $p'$. Below the triangle are the relations:\n$\\lambda = 1 - tg(x), \\quad \\lambda' = 1 - tg(x')$\n$d(F_t(x), F_t(x')) \\le |p - p'|$"
    }
  ],

{
  "edges": [
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The filling theorem extends Euclidean isoperimetric bounds from smooth manifolds with nonpositive sectional curvature to arbitrary proper metric spaces with nonpositive curvature and arbitrary cycle dimensions.",
      "relation": "generalises",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-337.2",
      "source_quote": "Every compactly supported $T\\in\\mathbf I_n(X)$ with $\\partial T=0$",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-337.1",
      "target_quote": "Every measurable set $E\\subset M$ with $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Vol}}_g(E)<\\infty$ and $P_g(E)<\\infty$ satisfies",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both results feature the sharp Euclidean isoperimetric constant relating boundary measure to enclosed volume for regions in nonpositively curved geometries.",
      "relation": "shared_constant",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-cor-cartan-hadamard",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-337.2",
      "source_quote": "The same inequality holds for relatively compact sets of finite perimeter, with ambient perimeter in place of boundary area.",
      "target_anchor": "thm:equality",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-337.1",
      "target_quote": "if and only if $E$ agrees up to ambient volume zero with an open region whose induced Riemannian metric is isometric to a round Euclidean ball.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    }
  ],
